
this is a task form an company for hiring i got. I want to do this step by step. 

1st giv eme clear idea in short what they want. than we will decide how we will start step by step. what will be the proecess.

What they want, in short
Build a production-style, highly available PostgreSQL cluster on Kubernetes without an operator (no CloudNativePG, Zalando, Crunchy, Bitnami HA chart, etc.), all as code. Then prove it works and document how you built it, including how you used AI.

The technical part
Area	What must be shown
Replication	1 primary + 2 replicas (PG 15+), streaming replication, replication slots with disk protection, lag measurement, and a justified sync vs async choice with its RPO
Connectivity	One stable read-write endpoint, one read-only endpoint, and automatic reconnection after failover (a pooler is optional)
Failover	Automatic promotion if the pod, node, or storage fails; split-brain prevention (fencing and leader election explained); the old primary rejoins automatically (pg_rewind or re-clone); a measured RTO
Backups	Scheduled base backups plus continuous WAL archiving to S3/GCS/MinIO, a retention policy, and automated backup verification
Restore	Full restore into an empty namespace, PITR to a timestamp, and an automated restore test
Operability	One command to bring everything up (make up) and one to tear it down; documented switchover and minor-version upgrade
Also graded	Security, fault tolerance (anti-affinity, spread constraints), and monitoring/alerting
Patroni or repmgr is allowed, as long as you deploy and wire it into Kubernetes yourself.

The AI-process part
This is half the assessment. They want:

Real, unedited session logs (only secrets removed)
Determinism: what is fixed (specs before code, acceptance tests, pinned versions, prompt templates) versus what depends on the model
Agent-agnostic method: vendor-specific parts kept separate
Generalisation: how the same playbook would apply to Redis Sentinel or Vault Raft, and which files you’d reuse or rewrite
Optional bonus: run one phase with a second AI agent and compare
Deliverables
GitHub repo with code, docs, and AI-process documentation
A 10–15 minute video covering approach, how to run it, and challenges
Email reply with links and hours spent, within 7 days
What gets you penalised
Using a forbidden operator or chart
Claims without evidence
Edited or reconstructed transcripts
Code you can’t explain
Hiding known gaps instead of documenting them
Their stated priority order
Replication and failover → backup/restore with PITR → monitoring/alerting → security hardening. Documenting what’s incomplete is acceptable.

Proposed process (for us to decide on)
Fix the decisions first: local cluster (kind or k3d, multi-node), Patroni vs DIY, and the backup tool (pgBackRest or WAL-G) with MinIO.
Write the spec and acceptance tests before any code: requirements as a checklist, each with a test (kill primary, measure RTO, restore, PITR).
Design doc: architecture, replication mode and RPO, fencing approach, and a threat model.
Build in priority order: cluster and replication, then failover, then backups and restore, then monitoring, then security.
Verify with evidence: run the failure tests and save the outputs and timings.
Package: the make up / make down entry point, docs, and the AI-process write-up.
Record the video and submit.
Throughout, we should keep logs from the start, since they have to be real and unedited. I’d suggest we save our conversation as we go and keep a short decisions log.

Before step 1, one question: do you want to use Patroni (the most common and defensible route, using the Kubernetes API for leader election) or build the failover logic yourself? I’d recommend Patroni. It is explicitly allowed, and a well-understood system you can defend beats a custom one.

i have wsl in my pc and before starting this things suggest me what are the best practices for this task like tools, architecture and everything

Best practices for this task
0. Fix your environment first
Move the repo into the WSL filesystem (~/[REDACTED:company]-pg-ha), not /mnt/c/.... Your current path on the Windows drive makes file I/O, git, and Docker bind mounts much slower, and it can cause permission problems with Postgres data.
Set limits in C:\Users\<you>\.wslconfig. A 4-node kind cluster running Postgres, MinIO, and Prometheus needs roughly 8-12 GB RAM and 4+ CPUs.
Use Docker with WSL integration (Docker Desktop, or Docker Engine inside WSL).
Use kind, not minikube. Multi-node is easy and it’s fast. A config with 1 control-plane and 3 workers lets you kill a “node” with docker stop, which is a realistic node-failure test.
Label the workers with fake zones (topology.kubernetes.io/zone=a/b/c) so your spread constraints are real, and explain what changes on a real multi-AZ cluster.
1. Recommended architecture
Concern	Choice	Why
Orchestrator	Patroni, using the Kubernetes API as its DCS	Explicitly allowed. Leader election uses a K8s lease or Endpoints object, which is easy to defend.
Workload	StatefulSet with 3 pods, one PVC each, plus headless service	Stable identity and storage per pod
Image	Build your own from the official postgres image plus pinned Patroni and pgBackRest	Avoid Zalando’s Spilo image. It’s their operator’s image, so it’s a gray area. Ask the recruiter if you want to use it.
Endpoints	pg-primary selects role=master, pg-replica selects role=replica	Patroni labels the pods, so failover re-routes with no client change
Replication	Physical streaming with Patroni-managed slots	Matches the brief
Sync vs async	synchronous_mode: true with 1 sync standby (RPO ≈ 0 while a sync standby exists)	Easy to justify. Document the degraded case and compare it with async plus maximum_lag_on_failover.
Disk protection	max_slot_wal_keep_size, plus an alert on slot retention	Required by the brief
Split-brain	Lease TTL: a primary that can’t renew its lease demotes itself. Optionally use a watchdog (not available in kind, so document that).	Explain fencing in the design doc
Rejoin	use_pg_rewind: true, with wal_log_hints or data checksums, and re-clone from pgBackRest as fallback	Automatic rejoin
Backups	pgBackRest to MinIO in-cluster, continuous WAL archiving, scheduled full and incremental backups via CronJob, retention set in config	Handles PITR, retention, and verification natively
Restore test	CronJob that restores to a scratch pod and checks known sentinel rows and pgbackrest verify	A backup that’s never been restored doesn’t count
Pooling	Skip at first. Add PgBouncer later only if time allows.	The brief says optional, and your priorities are elsewhere
Open decision: WAL-G is simpler to set up, but pgBackRest is stronger for PITR and retention. The tricky part is how the backup job reaches the data. Options are a sidecar or pgBackRest’s TLS server mode. We’ll decide that in the design step.

2. Fault tolerance, security, observability
Fault tolerance:

Pod anti-affinity and topologySpreadConstraints
A PodDisruptionBudget
Resource requests and limits, plus proper probes
A sensible terminationGracePeriodSeconds
Security:

Secrets generated at make up, never committed
scram-sha-256 and TLS
NetworkPolicies (kind’s default CNI may not enforce them, so use Calico or Cilium and verify)
Least-privilege RBAC for Patroni
Non-root containers and the restricted Pod Security Standard
Pinned image versions
gitleaks and trivy in CI
Observability:

kube-prometheus-stack (allowed, since it isn’t a Postgres operator)
postgres_exporter, Patroni’s /metrics, and a pgBackRest exporter
Alerts: no primary, replication lag, slot WAL retention, archive failures, backup too old, restore test failed
3. Tooling
kind, kubectl, helm (monitoring only), kustomize, make, shellcheck, kubeconform, pre-commit, gitleaks, and trivy. Write tests as plain bash scripts or bats, so any agent can run them.

4. Repo layout
AGENTS.md              # agent-agnostic instructions (CLAUDE.md just points here)
SPEC.md                # requirements + acceptance criteria, written BEFORE code
versions.env           # all pinned versions
Makefile               # up / down / test / chaos / restore-test
docs/decisions/        # short ADRs (sync vs async, fencing, backup tool...)
docs/design.md, runbook.md, threat-model.md, known-limitations.md
docs/ai-process/       # playbook, prompt templates, raw session logs
image/  k8s/  monitoring/  tests/  scripts/
5. The evidence you need
Every claim should link to saved output:

RTO: a write-probe that inserts a timestamped row every ~100 ms, then compute the gap during a primary kill. Run it for pod delete, node stop, and PVC loss.
Split-brain: partition the primary from the API and show it demotes.
PITR: insert data, note the timestamp, insert more data, restore to that timestamp, and diff.
Lag: show the query and the dashboard.
6. AI-process practices (half the grade)
Spec-driven: SPEC.md and acceptance tests come first, and every phase has a “done” gate (a passing test script).
Agent-agnostic: put the playbook in AGENTS.md and docs/ai-process/, and keep vendor-specific files thin and separate.
Logs: from today, export raw transcripts without editing them. Claude Code keeps session JSONL under ~/.claude/projects/. Redact secrets with a small committed script so the redaction itself is auditable.
Second agent for the bonus: run one phase (I’d pick the failover design or implementation) with Copilot or another CLI agent, then write up the differences.
Generalisation section: list which files transfer unchanged to Redis Sentinel or Vault Raft (SPEC template, AGENTS.md, test harness, Makefile skeleton) and which get rewritten (image, config, failover tests).
Never paste real credentials into any AI tool.
7. Suggested time budget (~12-15 h)
Phase	Hours
Spec, design, ADRs	1.5
Image, cluster, replication, services	3
Failover, fencing, rejoin, RTO tests	2.5
Backups, restore, PITR, verification	3
Monitoring and alerts	1.5
Security hardening	1
Docs, AI write-up, video	2

Next step: how much RAM does your laptop have, and is Docker working inside WSL? Once I know that, we’ll set up the environment and the repo skeleton, then write SPEC.md together.

Images hidden when shared
ok moving to pweorshell, my does not have docker desktop insatll anywhere. if nother ein the pdf about the image, then build we our own, why we nee cert manager and miniio ?

Short answers
Image: the PDF says nothing about images, so yes, we build our own. The only rule is no operators or turnkey HA charts. Our image will be the official postgres image plus pinned Patroni and pgBackRest. I’d avoid Zalando’s Spilo image, since it belongs to their operator ecosystem and could be read as a gray area.

MinIO is needed, cert-manager is not.

MinIO: the brief requires WAL archiving and base backups to object storage (S3, GCS, Azure or MinIO). A local kind cluster has no cloud bucket, so MinIO is the S3 stand-in that makes backups and PITR testable offline. If you used a real S3 bucket instead, you wouldn’t need it, but then a reviewer would need your cloud account to reproduce the setup. MinIO’s community packaging has changed recently, so we’ll check which image is pullable and pinnable. If it’s a problem, Garage or SeaweedFS are S3-compatible alternatives.
cert-manager: this is optional. It would automate TLS certificates for Postgres, Patroni and MinIO, including rotation. The simpler route is a script that generates certificates with openssl during make up and stores them as Secrets. I’d start with the script and only add cert-manager if time remains. Document the choice in an ADR.
Docker setup (the current blocker)
I’d skip Docker Desktop and install Docker Engine inside WSL. It’s lighter, and it’s the same environment the repo will live in. Also switch back to the WSL terminal, not PowerShell, because all the tooling will run there.

In PowerShell, run wsl -l -v. Check that your distro (Ubuntu) shows VERSION 2. Then run wsl to enter it.
Inside WSL, enable systemd. Run sudo vim /etc/wsl.conf and make sure it contains:
[boot]
systemd=true
Back in PowerShell, run wsl --shutdown, then open WSL again.
Install Docker Engine:
curl -fsSL https://get.docker.com | sh
sudo usermod -aG docker $USER
Then close and reopen the WSL terminal so the group change applies.
5. Verify with docker run --rm hello-world and docker info | grep -i "server version".

After that, we’ll install kind, kubectl and helm, and create the repo in ~/[REDACTED:company]-pg-ha.

Plan before we start
Your tool rule first: I’ll use tee with a heredoc for every file create or edit. One adjustment: sudo only for system files like /etc/wsl.conf. Files in ~/[REDACTED:company]-pg-ha should be created without sudo. Otherwise they become root-owned, and git, Docker builds and later edits will hit permission errors, which is also poor security practice. Example:

sudo tee /etc/wsl.conf > /dev/null <<'EOF'
[boot]
systemd=true
EOF
tee Makefile > /dev/null <<'EOF'
...
EOF
Use tee -a to append instead of overwrite.

Tool choices (all free and open source)
Need	Tool
Local cluster	kind (1 control-plane + 3 workers) with Calico, so NetworkPolicies are enforced
HA and failover	Patroni, with the Kubernetes API as its DCS
Backups, WAL archiving, PITR	pgBackRest
Object storage	An S3-compatible store in-cluster. MinIO’s community image situation has changed, so we verify what’s pullable, with Garage as the fallback
Monitoring	kube-prometheus-stack, postgres_exporter, Patroni metrics
Security scanning	trivy (images and manifests), gitleaks (secrets), kubeconform, kube-linter, shellcheck
TLS	openssl script at first, cert-manager only if time remains
Security practices we’ll follow
All versions pinned, and image digests where possible
Non-root containers, a read-only root filesystem where possible, dropped capabilities, and the restricted Pod Security Standard
scram-sha-256 and TLS for client and replication traffic
Secrets generated at make up, never committed, with .gitignore and gitleaks as a pre-commit hook
Least-privilege RBAC for Patroni, default-deny NetworkPolicies and explicit allow rules
Dedicated database roles, with no superuser used by applications
Every skipped hardening item recorded in known-limitations.md
Steps

Phase 1: Process setup (the AI-process half of the grade)
5. Create AGENTS.md, docs/ai-process/, and a decisions-log template
6. Set up raw session log capture before any real work, with a committed redaction script
7. Write SPEC.md with each requirement mapped to an acceptance test, then the ADRs for sync vs async, the backup tool, and fencing

Phase 2: Core (the highest priority)
8. Build the cluster with kind and Calico, with fake zone labels
9. Build the custom Postgres, Patroni and pgBackRest image
10. Deploy the StatefulSet, services (rw and ro), PDB, anti-affinity and spread constraints
11. Configure replication, slots and slot-disk protection, then lag measurement
12. Run failover tests: pod kill, node stop and PVC loss, with a write probe measuring RTO
13. Run split-brain and pg_rewind rejoin tests

Phase 3: Backup and restore
14. Deploy the object store and WAL archiving, with scheduled base backups and retention
15. Write the automated restore test, the full restore into a new namespace, and the PITR test

Phase 4: Observability and hardening
16. Deploy Prometheus, Grafana and alert rules for no primary, lag, slot retention, archive failure, stale backup and failed restore test
17. Add NetworkPolicies, TLS, RBAC review and image scans

Phase 5: Packaging
18. make up, make down, the switchover and minor-upgrade runbooks, and known-limitations.md
19. The generalisation section and the second-agent comparison (optional)
20. Video, then the submission email

Gate rule
Each phase ends with a passing test script, and we save its output as evidence. We don’t move on until it passes.

Step B: Git identity and SSH key (secure, no passwords)
Set your identity (use your real GitHub name and your GitHub no-reply email, so your personal email isn’t exposed in commits):
git config --global user.name "Your Name"
git config --global user.email "YOUR_ID+hmsmiraz@users.noreply.github.com"
git config --global init.defaultBranch main
You can find the no-reply address in GitHub → Settings → Emails.

Create an SSH key:
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "hmsmiraz-wsl" -f ~/.ssh/id_ed25519
Set a passphrase when asked.

Print the public key and copy it:
cat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub
In GitHub → Settings → SSH and GPG keys → New SSH key, paste it and save.
Test the connection:
ssh -T git@github.com
Type yes for the fingerprint prompt. You should see “Hi hmsmiraz! You’ve successfully authenticated”.

$user means? shold giv emy name?

No, leave $USER exactly as written. It’s a built-in shell variable that holds your current Linux username, so the shell fills it in for you. In your case it expands to hmsmiraz.

You can check it:

echo $USER
It should print hmsmiraz. The command then becomes sudo usermod -aG docker hmsmiraz, which adds you to the docker group so you can run docker without sudo.

Run both lines as they are:

curl -fsSL https://get.docker.com | sh
sudo usermod -aG docker $USER
The installer may print a warning about WSL and pause for a few seconds. That’s normal, so let it finish. It will ask for your sudo password.

Then close the terminal tab, open a new wsl terminal (the group change only applies to new sessions), and verify:

docker run --rm hello-world
free -h
Send me both outputs.

is it  running?

Yes, it’s running. It’s waiting for your sudo password.

The screen shows “WSL DETECTED... Docker Desktop” (just a recommendation, ignore it), then the 20-second pause, then [sudo: authenticate] Password:. Type your WSL/Ubuntu user password (the one you set when you created the Ubuntu user) and press Enter. The cursor won’t move and nothing will show while you type. That’s normal.

The sudo: preserving the entire environment is not supported line is harmless.

Then wait. The install takes 1-3 minutes and prints a lot of lines. It’s finished when you get your prompt back, usually after a line like “Client: Docker Engine - Community”.

If the password is rejected, you may have never set one. In that case, open PowerShell and run wsl -u root passwd hmsmiraz to set it, then retry.

When the prompt returns, run:

sudo usermod -aG docker $USER
The pasted sudo usermod line may already have run, but running it twice does no harm. Then close the terminal tab, open a new wsl terminal, and run:

You don’t need WSL for sudo tee. tee works anywhere. We use WSL because your tools (kind, kubectl, make, bash test scripts) are Linux tools, and the repo already lives there. Your VS Code window can also create and edit those files directly, so you can paste file contents into the editor instead of using tee.

Docker Desktop is fine. It’s free for personal use and skips the sudo problem. It uses WSL2 internally, so the only change is that you don’t install Docker yourself. Keep running commands in the wsl terminal, not PowerShell. The Makefile and bash scripts won’t run properly in PowerShell.

Install kubectl and kind (no sudo needed)
These go into ~/.local/bin. We record the exact versions in versions.env once they’re installed.

Create the folder and put it on your PATH:
mkdir -p ~/.local/bin
grep -q '.local/bin' ~/.bashrc || echo 'export PATH="$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"' >> ~/.bashrc
source ~/.bashrc
Install kubectl and verify its checksum:
KVER=$(curl -fsSL https://dl.k8s.io/release/stable.txt)
echo "$KVER"
curl -fsSLo ~/.local/bin/kubectl "https://dl.k8s.io/release/${KVER}/bin/linux/amd64/kubectl"
echo "$(curl -fsSL https://dl.k8s.io/release/${KVER}/bin/linux/amd64/kubectl.sha256)  $HOME/.local/bin/kubectl" | sha256sum --check
chmod +x ~/.local/bin/kubectl
The checksum line must print OK.
3. Install kind:

KIND_VER=$(curl -fsSL https://api.github.com/repos/kubernetes-sigs/kind/releases/latest | grep -m1 '"tag_name"' | cut -d'"' -f4)
echo "$KIND_VER"
curl -fsSLo ~/.local/bin/kind "https://kind.sigs.k8s.io/dl/${KIND_VER}/kind-linux-amd64"
chmod +x ~/.local/bin/kind
Verify:
kubectl version --client
kind version
Check whether make and jq are already installed. This also tells us if sudo is needed:
which make jq

Install gitleaks, trivy and shellcheck
gitleaks. This looks up the latest version first, because the file name contains it:
GL=$(curl -fsSL https://api.github.com/repos/gitleaks/gitleaks/releases/latest | jq -r .tag_name)

SC=$(curl -fsSL https://api.github.com/repos/koalaman/shellcheck/releases/latest | jq -r .tag_name)

shellcheck --version | head -2

Add a pre-commit hook that scans staged files for secrets. It lives in the repo, so reviewers can see it:
tee .githooks/pre-commit > /dev/null <<'EOF'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail
gitleaks git --pre-commit --staged --redact --verbose
EOF
chmod +x .githooks/pre-commit
git config core.hooksPath .githooks
Commit. The hook runs automatically, so you should see gitleaks report “no leaks found”:
git add -A

git push
Empty folders aren’t tracked by git, so only versions.env and .githooks/pre-commit will be in the commit. That’s expected.

Next: AGENTS.md
This is the agent-agnostic playbook. It says nothing vendor-specific, so any AI agent can follow it.

Create the file:
tee AGENTS.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# AGENTS.md: playbook for any AI agent working in this repo

## Goal
Highly available PostgreSQL on Kubernetes without a Postgres operator. Patroni for HA, pgBackRest for backup and PITR, kind for the local cluster. Requirements and acceptance tests are in SPEC.md.

## Rules
1. Read SPEC.md and docs/decisions/ before changing anything.
2. Spec first: no feature code before its acceptance test exists in tests/.
3. Everything is code. No manual kubectl exec steps in the build path.
4. Pin every version in versions.env. Never use `latest`.
5. No real credentials in files, prompts or logs. Secrets are generated at `make up`.
6. No Postgres operators and no turnkey HA charts (CloudNativePG, Zalando, Crunchy, StackGres, Percona, KubeBlocks, Bitnami postgresql-ha).
7. A phase is done only when its test script passes and the output is saved under docs/evidence/.
8. Record each design choice as a short ADR in docs/decisions/.
9. Document gaps in docs/known-limitations.md. Never hide them.
10. Run shellcheck on scripts and kubeconform on manifests before committing.

## Workflow per phase
1. State the goal and the acceptance test.
2. Write the test first and see it fail.
3. Implement the smallest change that passes.
4. Run the test and save the output.
5. Update docs and the ADR. Commit.

## Conventions
- Commit messages use conventional commits (feat, fix, docs, chore, test).
- Commits are authored by the human only. No co-author or contributor trailers.
- Shell scripts: bash, `set -euo pipefail`.
- Prefer plain bash tests so any agent can run them.
EOF
Commit it:
git add -A
git commit -m "docs: add agent-agnostic playbook (AGENTS.md)"
git push

Phase 1, step 1: session log redaction
How the logs work

docs/ai-process/raw/ holds the untouched logs and is never pushed (it’s in .gitignore).
scripts/redact-logs.sh copies each raw log into docs/ai-process/redacted/, replacing only secrets. It keeps the line count identical, so a reviewer can run diff raw redacted and see exactly which lines changed.
The script writes MANIFEST.tsv with the SHA-256 of every raw and redacted file plus the number of redacted lines. That is your evidence that the logs weren’t edited afterwards.
gitleaks scans the redacted folder as a second check.
1. Create the redaction script

tee scripts/redact-logs.sh > /dev/null <<'EOF'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Redact secrets from raw AI session logs.
# Only secrets are replaced. The line count is preserved, so
#   diff docs/ai-process/raw/X docs/ai-process/redacted/X
# shows exactly which lines were touched.
set -euo pipefail

RAW=docs/ai-process/raw
OUT=docs/ai-process/redacted
MANIFEST="$OUT/MANIFEST.tsv"

mkdir -p "$OUT"
printf 'file\traw_sha256\tredacted_sha256\tlines_redacted\n' > "$MANIFEST"

shopt -s nullglob
for f in "$RAW"/*; do
  [ -f "$f" ] || continue
  name=$(basename "$f")
  out="$OUT/$name"
  sed -E \
    -e '/-----BEGIN [A-Z ]*PRIVATE KEY-----/,/-----END [A-Z ]*PRIVATE KEY-----/ s/.*/[REDACTED:private-key-line]/' \
    -e 's/AKIA[0-9A-Z]{16}/[REDACTED:aws-access-key-id]/g' \
    -e 's/(gh[pousr]_[A-Za-z0-9]{36,}|github_pat_[A-Za-z0-9_]{20,})/[REDACTED:github-token]/g' \
    -e 's/xox[abprs]-[A-Za-z0-9-]{10,}/[REDACTED:slack-token]/g' \
    -e 's/(Bearer )[A-Za-z0-9._~+\/=-]{20,}/\1[REDACTED:bearer-token]/g' \
    -e 's#(://[^:/@[:space:]]+:)[^@[:space:]]+@#\1[REDACTED:uri-password]@#g' \
    -e 's/((password|passwd|secret|token|api[_-]?key|access[_-]?key)[[:space:]]*[=:][[:space:]]*)[^[:space:]]+/\1[REDACTED:value]/Ig' \
    "$f" > "$out"
  changed=$(diff "$f" "$out" | grep -c '^<' || true)
  printf '%s\t%s\t%s\t%s\n' "$name" \
    "$(sha256sum "$f" | cut -d' ' -f1)" \
    "$(sha256sum "$out" | cut -d' ' -f1)" "$changed" >> "$MANIFEST"
  echo "redacted $name: $changed line(s) changed"
done
EOF
chmod +x scripts/redact-logs.sh
shellcheck scripts/redact-logs.sh && echo "shellcheck OK"
2. Test it with fake secrets

These values are made up. Paste the whole block:

tee docs/ai-process/raw/test.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
hello world
export AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID=[REDACTED:aws-access-key-id]
db: postgres://app:[REDACTED:uri-password]@db.example:5432/app
password: notarealpassword
normal line
EOF
bash scripts/redact-logs.sh
cat docs/ai-process/redacted/test.md
cat docs/ai-process/redacted/MANIFEST.tsv
You should see 3 lines changed. “hello world” and “normal line” must be untouched, and the other three lines should show [REDACTED:...] markers.

3. Remove the test files

rm docs/ai-process/raw/test.md docs/ai-process/redacted/test.md docs/ai-process/redacted/MANIFEST.tsv
4. Document the logging rules

tee docs/ai-process/LOGGING.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# Session logging

## Rules
- Logs are saved verbatim in `docs/ai-process/raw/` (git-ignored). No edits, no summaries, no reconstruction.
- Only secrets are removed, by `scripts/redact-logs.sh`. The output goes to `docs/ai-process/redacted/` and is committed.
- `redacted/MANIFEST.tsv` records the SHA-256 of each raw and redacted file and the number of redacted lines.
- Line counts are preserved, so `diff raw/X redacted/X` shows only redacted lines.
- `gitleaks dir docs/ai-process/redacted` must report no leaks before committing.

## File naming
`YYYY-MM-DD-<tool>-<topic>.md`, for example `2026-11-19-claude-chat-setup.md`.

## What is captured
- Full chat text of every AI session that influenced the repo, saved at the end of each working session.
- Screenshots are not captured. Terminal output pasted into a chat is part of the text.

## Redaction limits
Pattern-based redaction cannot catch every secret, such as a password typed into the middle of a sentence. Secrets are never pasted into AI tools. The scripts only catch mistakes.
EOF
5. Commit

git add -A
git commit -m "chore: add log redaction script and logging rules"
git push

Next: prompt templates
These make the AI process repeatable. Every phase uses the same structure, and a different agent can follow it.

tee docs/ai-process/prompts/00-context.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# Context block (paste at the start of every session)

You are helping build: <ONE-LINE GOAL>.
Read first: AGENTS.md, SPEC.md, docs/decisions/*.
Constraints: no operators or turnkey charts, all versions pinned in versions.env, no real secrets in anything you read or write.
Output format: exact commands or file contents, one step at a time.
Ask before assuming anything that is not in SPEC.md.
EOF
tee docs/ai-process/prompts/01-spec.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# Template: turn requirements into a spec

Input: the requirement text for <AREA>.
Task: write numbered requirements (<AREA>-1, <AREA>-2, ...). For each one give:
- the requirement in one sentence
- an acceptance test: the command to run and the exact pass/fail condition
- the evidence file the test writes under docs/evidence/
Do not write implementation code.
EOF
tee docs/ai-process/prompts/02-design.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# Template: design decision (becomes an ADR)

Question: <DECISION>.
Task: list 2-3 options. For each give pros, cons, failure modes and security impact. Recommend one and say what evidence would change the recommendation.
Output: a draft ADR with Context, Options, Decision, Consequences.
The human decides. Do not implement.
EOF
tee docs/ai-process/prompts/03-implement.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# Template: implement one requirement

Requirement ID: <ID>. Its acceptance test already exists in tests/.
Task: write the smallest change that makes the test pass. Pin any new version in versions.env.
Output: file contents plus the commands to run, then the test command.
Run shellcheck and kubeconform on anything you wrote.
EOF
tee docs/ai-process/prompts/04-verify.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# Template: verify and record evidence

Run the acceptance test for <ID>. Save the raw output to docs/evidence/<ID>.txt.
Report: pass/fail, measured numbers with units, and any difference from the spec.
If it failed, explain the cause before changing code. Never edit a test to make it pass without recording why in an ADR.
EOF
Commit it:

git add -A
git commit -m "docs: add reusable prompt templates"
git push

SPEC.md
Each requirement has an ID, a test and an evidence file. The RTO figures are targets, and we’ll record the measured numbers later.

tee SPEC.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# SPEC: PostgreSQL HA on Kubernetes (no operator)

Stack: Patroni (Kubernetes API as DCS), pgBackRest, S3-compatible object store, kind + Calico.
Every requirement has a test in `tests/` and an evidence file in `docs/evidence/`.
Targets below are goals. Measured values go in the evidence files.

## REP: Replication
- REP-1: 1 primary + 2 replicas, PostgreSQL >= 15, physical streaming replication.
  Test: `tests/rep-topology.sh`. Pass: `patronictl list` shows 1 Leader + 2 streaming replicas.
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/REP-1.txt`
- REP-2: Synchronous replication with one sync standby. ADR-0001 states the RPO and the degraded case.
  Test: `tests/rep-sync.sh`. Pass: `pg_stat_replication` shows one `sync` row and a write is acknowledged only after the standby confirms.
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/REP-2.txt`
- REP-3: Replication slots with disk protection (`max_slot_wal_keep_size`).
  Test: `tests/rep-slots.sh`. Pass: slots exist for both replicas, and a stopped replica cannot grow WAL past the limit.
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/REP-3.txt`
- REP-4: Replication lag is measured.
  Test: `tests/rep-lag.sh`. Pass: lag in bytes and seconds is queried and appears as a Prometheus metric.
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/REP-4.txt`

## CON: Connectivity
- CON-1: Stable read-write service always routes to the current primary.
  Test: `tests/con-rw.sh`. Pass: `pg_is_in_recovery()` is false through the rw service, before and after failover.
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/CON-1.txt`
- CON-2: Separate read-only service routes to replicas only.
  Test: `tests/con-ro.sh`. Pass: `pg_is_in_recovery()` is true through the ro service, and writes are rejected.
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/CON-2.txt`
- CON-3: Clients reconnect after failover with no manual change.
  Test: the write probe in `tests/failover-*.sh`. Pass: probe resumes writing through the same endpoint.
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/CON-3.txt`

## FO: Failover
- FO-1: Primary pod killed, replica promoted automatically. Target RTO <= 60 s.
  Test: `tests/fo-pod-kill.sh`. Evidence: `docs/evidence/FO-1.txt` with the measured RTO.
- FO-2: Primary node lost (`docker stop` of the kind node). Target RTO <= 90 s.
  Test: `tests/fo-node-stop.sh`. Evidence: `docs/evidence/FO-2.txt`
- FO-3: Primary storage lost (PVC deleted). Replica promoted, the new pod re-clones.
  Test: `tests/fo-pvc-loss.sh`. Evidence: `docs/evidence/FO-3.txt`
- FO-4: No split-brain. A primary cut off from the Kubernetes API demotes itself, and two writable primaries never exist.
  Test: `tests/fo-split-brain.sh`. Pass: at no sampled moment do two pods report `pg_is_in_recovery() = false`.
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/FO-4.txt`
- FO-5: The old primary rejoins as a replica automatically (pg_rewind, re-clone as fallback).
  Test: `tests/fo-rejoin.sh`. Evidence: `docs/evidence/FO-5.txt`
- FO-6: RTO method: a probe inserts a timestamped row every 100 ms, and RTO is the largest gap between successful writes.
  Evidence: the FO-1 to FO-3 files.

## BK: Backups
- BK-1: Continuous WAL archiving to object storage.
  Test: `tests/bk-wal.sh`. Pass: `pgbackrest check` succeeds and new WAL objects appear.
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/BK-1.txt`
- BK-2: Scheduled full and incremental backups (CronJob).
  Test: `tests/bk-schedule.sh`. Evidence: `docs/evidence/BK-2.txt`
- BK-3: Retention enforced by pgBackRest settings.
  Test: `tests/bk-retention.sh`. Pass: backups beyond the limit are expired.
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/BK-3.txt`
- BK-4: Backups verified automatically (`pgbackrest verify` plus the restore test in RS-3).
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/BK-4.txt`

## RS: Restore
- RS-1: Full cluster restore into an empty namespace by one script.
  Test: `tests/rs-full.sh`. Pass: restored data matches the sentinel dataset.
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/RS-1.txt`
- RS-2: Point-in-time recovery to a timestamp.
  Test: `tests/rs-pitr.sh`. Pass: rows written before the target time exist, rows written after do not.
  Evidence: `docs/evidence/RS-2.txt`
- RS-3: Scheduled restore test to a scratch instance, with a data check.
  Test: `tests/rs-scheduled.sh`. Evidence: `docs/evidence/RS-3.txt`

## OPS: Operability
- OPS-1: `make up` brings up everything. Test: from a clean machine, `make up && make test` passes.
- OPS-2: `make down` removes everything. Pass: no kind cluster and no leftover volumes.
- OPS-3: Runbooks for planned switchover and minor-version upgrade in `docs/runbook.md`.

## SEC: Security
- SEC-1: Secrets generated at `make up`, none committed. Test: gitleaks on the repo and history.
- SEC-2: `scram-sha-256` and TLS for client and replication traffic.
- SEC-3: Default-deny NetworkPolicies with explicit allows. Test: a blocked connection is demonstrated.
- SEC-4: Least-privilege RBAC for Patroni.
- SEC-5: Non-root containers, dropped capabilities, restricted Pod Security Standard.
- SEC-6: Image scan with trivy. Evidence: `docs/evidence/SEC-6.txt`

## OBS: Observability
- OBS-1: Prometheus scrapes postgres_exporter and Patroni metrics.
- OBS-2: Alerts: no primary, replication lag, slot retention, WAL archive failure, backup too old, restore test failed.
- OBS-3: Each alert is triggered once on purpose, and the firing is saved as evidence.

## FT: Fault tolerance
- FT-1: Pod anti-affinity and topology spread across nodes and fake zones.
- FT-2: PodDisruptionBudget, resource requests and limits, probes, and a termination grace period.

## DOC: Documentation
- DOC-1: `docs/design.md`, `docs/runbook.md`, `docs/known-limitations.md`, ADRs, and the AI-process docs.
EOF
Commit it:

git commit -m "docs: add SPEC with requirement IDs and acceptance tests"

1. Create the five prompt templates

tee docs/ai-process/prompts/00-context.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# Context block (paste at the start of every session)

You are helping build: <ONE-LINE GOAL>.
Read first: AGENTS.md, SPEC.md, docs/decisions/*.
Constraints: no operators or turnkey charts, all versions pinned in versions.env, no real secrets in anything you read or write.
Output format: exact commands or file contents, one step at a time.
Ask before assuming anything that is not in SPEC.md.
EOF

tee docs/ai-process/prompts/01-spec.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# Template: turn requirements into a spec

Input: the requirement text for <AREA>.
Task: write numbered requirements (<AREA>-1, <AREA>-2, ...). For each one give:
- the requirement in one sentence
- an acceptance test: the command to run and the exact pass/fail condition
- the evidence file the test writes under docs/evidence/
Do not write implementation code.
EOF

tee docs/ai-process/prompts/02-design.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# Template: design decision (becomes an ADR)

Question: <DECISION>.
Task: list 2-3 options. For each give pros, cons, failure modes and security impact. Recommend one and say what evidence would change the recommendation.
Output: a draft ADR with Context, Options, Decision, Consequences.
The human decides. Do not implement.
EOF

tee docs/ai-process/prompts/03-implement.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# Template: implement one requirement

Requirement ID: <ID>. Its acceptance test already exists in tests/.
Task: write the smallest change that makes the test pass. Pin any new version in versions.env.
Output: file contents plus the commands to run, then the test command.
Run shellcheck and kubeconform on anything you wrote.
EOF

tee docs/ai-process/prompts/04-verify.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# Template: verify and record evidence

Run the acceptance test for <ID>. Save the raw output to docs/evidence/<ID>.txt.
Report: pass/fail, measured numbers with units, and any difference from the spec.
If it failed, explain the cause before changing code. Never edit a test to make it pass without recording why in an ADR.
EOF

The four ADRs
Each ADR has a status, and the object store and the backup invocation are marked as still open, so they don’t claim anything we haven’t tested. Paste each block separately.

tee docs/decisions/0001-sync-vs-async-replication.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# ADR-0001: Synchronous replication with one sync standby

Status: Accepted

## Context
The brief asks us to choose between synchronous and asynchronous replication and to state the RPO.

## Options
1. Asynchronous. Fastest commits. A failover can lose the last commits that had not reached a replica, so the RPO is greater than zero and depends on lag.
2. Synchronous, strict mode. RPO is 0 for acknowledged commits. If no sync standby is available, writes block.
3. Synchronous, non-strict mode (Patroni `synchronous_mode: true`, `synchronous_mode_strict: false`, `synchronous_node_count: 1`). RPO is 0 while a sync standby exists. If every replica is down, Patroni falls back to asynchronous so the primary keeps accepting writes.

## Decision
Option 3. With synchronous mode on, Patroni only promotes the current sync standby, so a promoted node has every acknowledged commit.

## Consequences
- RPO is 0 for acknowledged commits while at least one sync standby is healthy.
- During the degraded state (no sync standby), the RPO is bounded only by replication lag. This is a documented risk, and the alert "no synchronous standby" covers it.
- Commit latency includes one network round trip to the standby, and a slow standby slows writes.
- To be measured and recorded in `docs/evidence/REP-2.txt`: commit latency with and without synchronous mode.
EOF
tee docs/decisions/0002-backup-tool.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# ADR-0002: pgBackRest for backups, WAL archiving and PITR

Status: Accepted for the tool. Open for how scheduled backups reach the data (decided in Phase 3).

## Context
We need base backups, continuous WAL archiving to object storage, retention, verification and point-in-time recovery.

## Options
1. pgBackRest. Full, differential and incremental backups, retention settings, `verify`, PITR and S3 support in one tool.
2. WAL-G. Simpler to configure and fast, but retention and verification are less complete.
3. pg_basebackup plus a custom archive script. Most work, and we would own every edge case.

## Decision
pgBackRest. `archive_command` in the Postgres pods pushes WAL to the S3-compatible repository. Retention is declared in the pgBackRest configuration.

## Open question
How the scheduled backup job reaches the cluster: a CronJob using pgBackRest TLS server mode, or a sidecar. We decide this when we build Phase 3 and update this ADR.

## Consequences
- The image includes a pinned pgBackRest version.
- Backup, verify and restore scripts all use the same tool.
- Repository credentials are Kubernetes Secrets generated at `make up`.
EOF
tee docs/decisions/0003-leader-election-and-fencing.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# ADR-0003: Patroni with the Kubernetes API for leader election and fencing

Status: Accepted

## Context
Two primaries must never accept writes at the same time. The brief asks us to explain the fencing approach and the election mechanism.

## Options
1. Patroni with the Kubernetes API as its DCS. The leader key lives in a Kubernetes object, and the leader renews it every loop.
2. Patroni with etcd or Consul. A separate consensus cluster to run, secure and back up.
3. A custom controller. Most flexible and hardest to defend.

## Decision
Option 1.

- Election: a pod acquires the leader key through the Kubernetes API, which is backed by etcd. Only one pod can hold it.
- Lease: `ttl: 30`, `loop_wait: 10`, `retry_timeout: 10`, so `loop_wait + 2 * retry_timeout <= ttl`. A leader that cannot renew the key within the TTL demotes itself.
- Fencing: software based. A primary cut off from the API demotes itself (stops accepting writes) before the TTL expires and another pod can take over. There is no STONITH.
- Extra protection: with synchronous mode on (ADR-0001), a stale primary that keeps running cannot get a sync acknowledgment from the standby that is now following the new primary, so its commits block.
- Rejoin: the old primary uses pg_rewind (needs `wal_log_hints` or data checksums), and re-clones from the new primary if rewind fails.

## Consequences
- A hung Patroni process on a partitioned node is not covered by a hardware watchdog, because kind has none. Documented in `known-limitations.md`. On real nodes we would enable a watchdog.
- The pods need RBAC on the leader and config objects only.
- Failover time depends on the TTL, which trades faster failover against more false failovers on a busy API server.
EOF
tee docs/decisions/0004-object-store.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# ADR-0004: S3-compatible object store inside the cluster

Status: Proposed. Final choice after checking which image is pullable and pinnable (Phase 3).

## Context
The brief requires backups and WAL archives in object storage. A reviewer must be able to reproduce the setup without a cloud account.

## Options
1. MinIO in the cluster. Well known S3 API, easy to run for tests.
2. Garage. Small, S3-compatible, open source.
3. A real cloud bucket (S3 or GCS). Closest to production, but needs the reviewer's account and credentials.

## Decision
Start with MinIO if a pinned image can be pulled. Fall back to Garage if not. A cloud bucket is the production recommendation, documented but not used here.

## Consequences
- pgBackRest talks to a single S3 endpoint setting, so moving to a cloud bucket later changes configuration only.
- The in-cluster store has no durability beyond the cluster. A single in-cluster store is not a real backup target, and this goes in `known-limitations.md`.
- Bucket credentials are generated at `make up`.
EOF

1. Create the four ADRs
tee docs/decisions/0001-sync-vs-async-replication.md > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# ADR-0001: Synchronous replication with one sync standby

Status: Accepted

## Context
The brief asks us to choose between synchronous and asynchronous replication and to state the RPO.

## Options
1. Asynchronous. Fastest commits. A failover can lose the last commits that had not reached a replica, so the RPO is greater than zero and depends on lag.
2. Synchronous, strict mode. RPO is 0 for acknowledged commits. If no sync standby is available, writes block.
3. Synchronous, non-strict mode (Patroni `synchronous_mode: true`, `synchronous_mode_strict: false`, `synchronous_node_count: 1`). RPO is 0 while a sync standby exists. If every replica is down, Patroni falls back to asynchronous so the primary keeps accepting writes.

## Decision
Option 3. With synchronous mode on, Patroni only promotes the current sync standby, so a promoted node has every acknowledged commit.

## Consequences
- RPO is 0 for acknowledged commits while at least one sync standby is healthy.
- During the degraded state (no sync standby), the RPO is bounded only by replication lag. This is a documented risk, and the alert "no synchronous standby" covers it.
- Commit latency includes one network round trip to the standby, and a slow standby slows writes.
- To be measured and recorded in `docs/evidence/REP-2.txt`: commit latency with and without synchronous mode.
EOF

4. Write the acceptance test first. It checks that 4 nodes are Ready, that the workers are in 3 fake zones, that Calico is running, and that a default-deny policy actually blocks traffic.

tee tests/env-cluster.sh > /dev/null <<'EOF'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Acceptance test: kind cluster, zones, Calico, NetworkPolicy enforcement.
set -euo pipefail

pass() { echo "PASS: $*"; }
fail() { echo "FAIL: $*"; exit 1; }
ns=netpol-test
trap 'kubectl delete ns "$ns" --ignore-not-found --wait=false >/dev/null 2>&1 || true' EXIT

echo "date: $(date -u +%FT%TZ)"
kubectl get nodes -o wide

nodes=$(kubectl get nodes --no-headers | wc -l)
[ "$nodes" -eq 4 ] || fail "expected 4 nodes, got $nodes"
notready=$(kubectl get nodes --no-headers | awk '$2 != "Ready"' | wc -l)
[ "$notready" -eq 0 ] || fail "$notready node(s) not Ready"
pass "4 nodes Ready"

zones=$(kubectl get nodes -l '!node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane' \
  -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.labels.topology\.kubernetes\.io/zone}{"\n"}{end}' | sort -u | grep -c .)
[ "$zones" -eq 3 ] || fail "expected 3 distinct worker zones, got $zones"
pass "3 workers in 3 distinct zones"

kubectl -n kube-system rollout status ds/calico-node --timeout=120s >/dev/null || fail "calico-node not ready"
pass "calico-node running on every node"

kubectl delete ns "$ns" --ignore-not-found --wait=true >/dev/null
kubectl create ns "$ns" >/dev/null
kubectl -n "$ns" run server --image=busybox:1.37.0 --restart=Never --port=8080 -- \
  sh -c 'mkdir -p /www && echo ok > /www/index.html && httpd -f -p 8080 -h /www' >/dev/null
kubectl -n "$ns" expose pod server --port=8080 >/dev/null
kubectl -n "$ns" wait --for=condition=Ready pod/server --timeout=120s >/dev/null

probe() {
  timeout 90 kubectl -n "$ns" run "client-$RANDOM" --rm -i --restart=Never \
    --image=busybox:1.37.0 -- wget -qO- -T 3 http://server:8080 >/dev/null 2>&1
}

probe || fail "baseline: client cannot reach server before any policy"
pass "baseline: client reaches server"

kubectl -n "$ns" apply -f - >/dev/null <<'YAML'
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: default-deny-ingress
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes: ["Ingress"]
YAML
sleep 5

if probe; then
  fail "NetworkPolicy is NOT enforced: client still reaches server"
fi
pass "NetworkPolicy enforced: traffic blocked after default-deny"
echo "ALL CHECKS PASSED"
EOF
chmod +x tests/env-cluster.sh
5. Write the cluster script. It’s the first piece of make up.

tee scripts/cluster-up.sh > /dev/null <<'EOF'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Create the kind cluster, install Calico, label workers with fake zones.
set -euo pipefail
cd "$(dirname "$0")/.."
# shellcheck source=/dev/null
source versions.env

CLUSTER=kind
if kind get clusters 2>/dev/null | grep -qx "$CLUSTER"; then
  echo "kind cluster '$CLUSTER' already exists, reusing it"
else
  kind create cluster --name "$CLUSTER" \
    --config k8s/kind-config.yaml \
    --image "kindest/node:${KIND_NODE_IMAGE}"
fi
kubectl config use-context "kind-${CLUSTER}" >/dev/null

kubectl apply -f "k8s/calico/calico-${CALICO_VERSION}.yaml"
kubectl -n kube-system rollout status ds/calico-node --timeout=300s
kubectl -n kube-system rollout status deploy/calico-kube-controllers --timeout=300s
kubectl wait --for=condition=Ready nodes --all --timeout=300s

zones=(zone-a zone-b zone-c)
i=0
for n in $(kubectl get nodes -l '!node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane' -o name | sort); do
  kubectl label --overwrite "$n" "topology.kubernetes.io/zone=${zones[$i]}"
  i=$((i + 1))
done
kubectl get nodes -L topology.kubernetes.io/zone
EOF
chmod +x scripts/cluster-up.sh
shellcheck scripts/cluster-up.sh tests/env-cluster.sh && echo "shellcheck OK"

The cluster is still running, so we diagnose it instead of rebuilding.

1. See what the Calico pods are doing
kubectl -n kube-system get pods -o wide
2. See why
kubectl -n kube-system describe pod -l k8s-app=calico-node | grep -A12 -i "^Events:" | head -40
kubectl -n kube-system get events --sort-by=.lastTimestamp | tail -15
What to look for in the pod status:

Init:0/3 or ImagePullBackOff: the nodes are still downloading the Calico images, or the pull is failing. This is common on a slow connection, because each of the 4 nodes pulls the images on its own. It would not be a Kubernetes 1.37 problem, and we have a fix below.
CrashLoopBackOff or Running with 0/1 ready: a runtime or compatibility problem. Then I need the logs:
kubectl -n kube-system logs ds/calico-node -c calico-node --tail=30
3. If it is a slow pull, don’t wait it out
Check whether the images are still downloading:

kubectl -n kube-system get pods -w
Stop the watch with Ctrl+C after a minute. If the pods move from Init:0/3 to Init:1/3 and on, the pull is only slow. Re-run the script and it will wait again:

bash scripts/cluster-up.sh
It reuses the cluster, and the apply step is safe to repeat. If the pull keeps failing, I’ll give you a fix that pulls the images once on your host and loads them into every kind node, which works because we turned off the containerd image store earlier.

3. Fix the script so this doesn’t happen again
A reviewer on a slow connection would hit the same timeout, so the script should pull each image once on the host and load it into all nodes. Replace the script (this is safe to do while the cluster is up):

tee scripts/cluster-up.sh > /dev/null <<'EOF'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Create the kind cluster, install Calico, label workers with fake zones.
set -euo pipefail
cd "$(dirname "$0")/.."
# shellcheck source=/dev/null
source versions.env

CLUSTER=kind
MANIFEST="k8s/calico/calico-${CALICO_VERSION}.yaml"

if kind get clusters 2>/dev/null | grep -qx "$CLUSTER"; then
  echo "kind cluster '$CLUSTER' already exists, reusing it"
else
  kind create cluster --name "$CLUSTER" \
    --config k8s/kind-config.yaml \
    --image "kindest/node:${KIND_NODE_IMAGE}"
fi
kubectl config use-context "kind-${CLUSTER}" >/dev/null

# Pull each Calico image once on the host and load it into every node,
# instead of all 4 nodes pulling the same images over one connection.
mapfile -t images < <(grep -E '^\s+image:' "$MANIFEST" | awk '{print $2}' | tr -d '"' | sort -u)
for img in "${images[@]}"; do
  docker image inspect "$img" >/dev/null 2>&1 || docker pull "$img"
  kind load docker-image "$img" --name "$CLUSTER"
done

kubectl apply -f "$MANIFEST"
kubectl -n kube-system rollout status ds/calico-node --timeout=600s
kubectl -n kube-system rollout status deploy/calico-kube-controllers --timeout=600s
kubectl wait --for=condition=Ready nodes --all --timeout=600s

zones=(zone-a zone-b zone-c)
i=0
for n in $(kubectl get nodes -l '!node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane' -o name | sort); do
  kubectl label --overwrite "$n" "topology.kubernetes.io/zone=${zones[$i]}"
  i=$((i + 1))
done
kubectl get nodes -L topology.kubernetes.io/zone
EOF
shellcheck scripts/cluster-up.sh && echo "shellcheck OK"
Don’t run it while the pods are still pulling, since both would compete for bandwidth. Do step 1 and 2 first. The new script is for the next make up from scratch, and we’ll check that it works when we build make up.

Images hidden when shared
stuck here

It’s probably not frozen. The first three checks passed, and the test is now on the NetworkPolicy check. That check starts a busybox server pod and later a client pod, and each node has to download the image first. Your connection took 5 minutes for the Calico images, so this can take a while too. The test waits up to 120 s for the server pod and 90 s per client probe, so a slow pull can also make it fail with a timeout.

1. See what it is doing
Open a second wsl terminal (the + in the terminal panel) and run:

kubectl -n netpol-test get pods -o wide
ContainerCreating: the image is downloading. Wait a few minutes.
ErrImagePull or ImagePullBackOff: the pull failed. Send me the output.
Running: the pull finished, so the test should move on within seconds.
To see the pull progress:

kubectl -n netpol-test describe pod server | tail -8

It was not the image pull, so my last guess was wrong. The image downloaded in 8 seconds. The client-29219 pod is Completed, which means its wget succeeded, so the baseline check passed. The test is hung on how it waits for the pod: kubectl run --rm -i attached to the pod and never returned, even though the pod had already finished. This is a bug in my test script, and not a Calico or cluster problem. The server and client show the pods can talk, so the cluster is fine.

The fix is to stop attaching. The new probe creates the pod, polls its phase until it reaches Succeeded or Failed, and deletes it.

1. Stop the hung test and clean up
Click in the stuck terminal and press Ctrl+C, then:

kubectl delete ns netpol-test --ignore-not-found
2. Replace the test script
tee tests/env-cluster.sh > /dev/null <<'EOF'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Acceptance test: kind cluster, zones, Calico, NetworkPolicy enforcement.
set -euo pipefail

pass() { echo "PASS: $*"; }
fail() { echo "FAIL: $*"; exit 1; }
ns=netpol-test
trap 'kubectl delete ns "$ns" --ignore-not-found --wait=false >/dev/null 2>&1 || true' EXIT

echo "date: $(date -u +%FT%TZ)"
kubectl get nodes -o wide

nodes=$(kubectl get nodes --no-headers | wc -l)
[ "$nodes" -eq 4 ] || fail "expected 4 nodes, got $nodes"
notready=$(kubectl get nodes --no-headers | awk '$2 != "Ready"' | wc -l)
[ "$notready" -eq 0 ] || fail "$notready node(s) not Ready"
pass "4 nodes Ready"

zones=$(kubectl get nodes -l '!node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane' \
  -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.labels.topology\.kubernetes\.io/zone}{"\n"}{end}' | sort -u | grep -c .)
[ "$zones" -eq 3 ] || fail "expected 3 distinct worker zones, got $zones"
pass "3 workers in 3 distinct zones"

kubectl -n kube-system rollout status ds/calico-node --timeout=120s >/dev/null || fail "calico-node not ready"
pass "calico-node running on every node"

kubectl delete ns "$ns" --ignore-not-found --wait=true >/dev/null
kubectl create ns "$ns" >/dev/null
kubectl -n "$ns" run server --image=busybox:1.37.0 --restart=Never --port=8080 -- \
  sh -c 'mkdir -p /www && echo ok > /www/index.html && httpd -f -p 8080 -h /www' >/dev/null
kubectl -n "$ns" expose pod server --port=8080 >/dev/null
kubectl -n "$ns" wait --for=condition=Ready pod/server --timeout=120s >/dev/null

# Run a client pod without attaching, then read its final phase.
# Succeeded = wget got a reply. Failed = wget timed out or was refused.
probe() {
  local name="client-$RANDOM" phase=""
  kubectl -n "$ns" run "$name" --image=busybox:1.37.0 --restart=Never -- \
    wget -qO- -T 3 http://server:8080 >/dev/null
  for _ in $(seq 1 60); do
    phase=$(kubectl -n "$ns" get pod "$name" -o jsonpath='{.status.phase}')
    case "$phase" in
      Succeeded) kubectl -n "$ns" delete pod "$name" --wait=false >/dev/null; return 0 ;;
      Failed)    kubectl -n "$ns" delete pod "$name" --wait=false >/dev/null; return 1 ;;
    esac
    sleep 2
  done
  kubectl -n "$ns" delete pod "$name" --wait=false >/dev/null
  return 1
}

probe || fail "baseline: client cannot reach server before any policy"
pass "baseline: client reaches server"

kubectl -n "$ns" apply -f - >/dev/null <<'YAML'
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: default-deny-ingress
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes: ["Ingress"]
YAML
sleep 5

if probe; then
  fail "NetworkPolicy is NOT enforced: client still reaches server"
fi
pass "NetworkPolicy enforced: traffic blocked after default-deny"
echo "ALL CHECKS PASSED"
EOF
shellcheck tests/env-cluster.sh && echo "shellcheck OK"
3. Run it and save the evidence
bash tests/env-cluster.sh 2>&1 | tee docs/evidence/ENV-cluster.txt
It should finish in under a minute and end with ALL CHECKS PASSED. This overwrites the partial evidence file from the hung run.

hmsmiraz@HMSMIRAZ:~/postgres-ha-lab$ tee tests/env-cluster.sh > /dev/null <<'EOF'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Acceptance test: kind cluster, zones, Calico, NetworkPolicy enforcement.
set -euo pipefail

pass() { echo "PASS: $*"; }
fail() { echo "FAIL: $*"; exit 1; }
ns=netpol-test
trap 'kubectl delete ns "$ns" --ignore-not-found --wait=false >/dev/null 2>&1 || true' EXIT

echo "date: $(date -u +%FT%TZ)"
kubectl get nodes -o wide

nodes=$(kubectl get nodes --no-headers | wc -l)
[ "$nodes" -eq 4 ] || fail "expected 4 nodes, got $nodes"
notready=$(kubectl get nodes --no-headers | awk '$2 != "Ready"' | wc -l)
[ "$notready" -eq 0 ] || fail "$notready node(s) not Ready"
pass "4 nodes Ready"

zones=$(kubectl get nodes -l '!node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane' \
  -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.labels.topology\.kubernetes\.io/zone}{"\n"}{end}' | sort -u | grep -c .)
[ "$zones" -eq 3 ] || fail "expected 3 distinct worker zones, got $zones"
pass "3 workers in 3 distinct zones"

kubectl -n kube-system rollout status ds/calico-node --timeout=120s >/dev/null || fail "calico-node not ready"
pass "calico-node running on every node"

kubectl delete ns "$ns" --ignore-not-found --wait=true >/dev/null
kubectl create ns "$ns" >/dev/null
kubectl -n "$ns" run server --image=busybox:1.37.0 --restart=Never --port=8080 -- \
  sh -c 'mkdir -p /www && echo ok > /www/index.html && httpd -f -p 8080 -h /www' >/dev/null
kubectl -n "$ns" expose pod server --port=8080 >/dev/null
kubectl -n "$ns" wait --for=condition=Ready pod/server --timeout=120s >/dev/null

# Run a client pod without attaching, then read its final phase.
# Succeeded = wget got a reply. Failed = wget timed out or was refused.
probe() {
  local name="client-$RANDOM" phase=""
  kubectl -n "$ns" run "$name" --image=busybox:1.37.0 --restart=Never -- \
    wget -qO- -T 3 http://server:8080 >/dev/null
  for _ in $(seq 1 60); do
    phase=$(kubectl -n "$ns" get pod "$name" -o jsonpath='{.status.phase}')
    case "$phase" in
      Succeeded) kubectl -n "$ns" delete pod "$name" --wait=false >/dev/null; return 0 ;;
      Failed)    kubectl -n "$ns" delete pod "$name" --wait=false >/dev/null; return 1 ;;
    esac
    sleep 2
  done
  kubectl -n "$ns" delete pod "$name" --wait=false >/dev/null
  return 1
}

probe || fail "baseline: client cannot reach server before any policy"
pass "baseline: client reaches server"

kubectl -n "$ns" apply -f - >/dev/null <<'YAML'
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: default-deny-ingress
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes: ["Ingress"]
YAML
sleep 5

if probe; then
  fail "NetworkPolicy is NOT enforced: client still reaches server"
fi
pass "NetworkPolicy enforced: traffic blocked after default-deny"
echo "ALL CHECKS PASSED"
EOF
shellcheck tests/env-cluster.sh && echo "shellcheck OK"
shellcheck OK
hmsmiraz@HMSMIRAZ:~/postgres-ha-lab$ grep -c "Run a client pod without attaching" tests/env-cluster.sh
1
hmsmiraz@HMSMIRAZ:~/postgres-ha-lab$

Here’s what I found, and what I’ll build the image from. All versions come from the release pages, and the exact package versions and digests will come from your machine so nothing is guessed.

Component	Version	Why
PostgreSQL	18.7	Released 2026-11-12 and fixes 52 CVEs, so the minimum I’d build on. PostgreSQL 19 GA was announced for today, but I’d stay on 18 for the main build. The brief only requires 15 or newer.
Patroni	4.1.5	Newest release I found (2026-08-12).
pgBackRest	2.60.0	Released 2026-10-18. It includes the fix for weak encryption keys that came in 2.59.3.
Image plan: the official postgres:18.7-trixie image as base, Patroni installed in a Python venv from a locked requirements file, and pgBackRest from the PGDG apt repo that the official image already uses. It runs as the postgres user, not root.

docker run --rm postgres:18.7-trixie bash -c 'apt-get update -qq >/dev/null 2>&1; apt-cache madison pgbackrest'
The first command prints a line like postgres@sha256:... (the digest we’ll pin). The second prints the available pgbackrest package versions, and I need the line that contains 2.60.0.

4. Create the locked Python requirements
This installs Patroni once in a throwaway Python 3.13 container (the same Python as trixie) and saves the exact versions of everything it pulled in:

docker run --rm python:3.13-slim-trixie bash -c 'pip install -q --root-user-action=ignore "patroni[kubernetes]==4.1.5" psycopg2-binary && pip freeze' | tee image/requirements.lock
The file should list patroni==4.1.5 and psycopg2-binary==... plus their dependencies. If the command prints an error instead, send it to me.

Here’s where we stand:

Commit c08a4b0 is pushed: the cluster step, with the passing and the failing evidence files.
Base image digest: sha256:e754b839... is recorded and will be pinned.
Python lock: it captured 16 packages, including patroni==4.1.5 and psycopg2-binary==2.9.13. The pip upgrade notice is harmless.
pgBackRest 2.60.1: the PGDG repo has 2.60.1 as well as 2.60.0. I haven’t read the 2.60.1 release notes, so I’m pinning 2.60.0-1.pgdg13+1, the version I verified. Moving to 2.60.1 later is a one-line change in versions.env.
Phase 2, step 2: the image
As with the cluster, the test comes first, so you’ll see it fail before the image exists.

1. Pin the image versions

tee -a versions.env > /dev/null <<'EOF'
POSTGRES_BASE_IMAGE=postgres:18.7-trixie@sha256:e754b839f447639b7004895f4d8a4bfcb4e9dbe3a1bc035f27f646562a16fd61
PGBACKREST_APT_VERSION=2.60.0-1.pgdg13+1
IMAGE_NAME=pg-ha
IMAGE_TAG=18.7-patroni4.1.5-pgbackrest2.60.0
EOF
2. Keep the build context minimal. Only the lock file gets sent to Docker, so no secrets or logs can leak into an image.

tee .dockerignore > /dev/null <<'EOF'
*
!image/requirements.lock
EOF
3. Create the Dockerfile

tee image/Dockerfile > /dev/null <<'EOF'
# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1
ARG BASE_IMAGE
FROM ${BASE_IMAGE}

ARG PGBACKREST_APT_VERSION
ARG PATRONI_VERSION
ARG POSTGRES_VERSION

LABEL org.opencontainers.image.title="pg-ha" \
      org.opencontainers.image.description="PostgreSQL with Patroni and pgBackRest" \
      org.opencontainers.image.base.name="${BASE_IMAGE}"

# pgBackRest from the PGDG repo (already configured in the official image),
# tini as PID 1 so zombie processes are reaped, python for Patroni.
RUN apt-get update \
 && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends \
      python3 python3-venv tini \
      "pgbackrest=${PGBACKREST_APT_VERSION}" \
 && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*

# Patroni and its dependencies from the committed lock file, in a venv.
COPY image/requirements.lock /tmp/requirements.lock
RUN python3 -m venv /opt/patroni \
 && /opt/patroni/bin/pip install --no-cache-dir --require-virtualenv -r /tmp/requirements.lock \
 && rm /tmp/requirements.lock

ENV PATH="/opt/patroni/bin:${PATH}"

RUN mkdir -p /pgdata /etc/patroni \
 && chown postgres:postgres /pgdata \
 && chmod 700 /pgdata

USER postgres
WORKDIR /home/postgres

# Patroni starts and stops Postgres itself, so we do not use the stock entrypoint.
ENTRYPOINT ["tini", "--", "patroni"]
CMD ["/etc/patroni/patroni.yml"]
EOF
4. Write the acceptance test first

tee tests/image-build.sh > /dev/null <<'EOF'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Acceptance test: the custom image has the pinned tools and runs as non-root.
set -euo pipefail
cd "$(dirname "$0")/.."
# shellcheck source=/dev/null
source versions.env

IMG="${IMAGE_NAME}:${IMAGE_TAG}"
pass() { echo "PASS: $*"; }
fail() { echo "FAIL: $*"; exit 1; }
run() { docker run --rm --entrypoint "$@"; }

echo "date: $(date -u +%FT%TZ)"
echo "image: $IMG"

docker image inspect "$IMG" >/dev/null 2>&1 || fail "image $IMG does not exist"
pass "image exists"

uid=$(run id "$IMG" -u)
[ "$uid" != "0" ] || fail "image runs as root"
pass "runs as non-root (uid $uid)"

out=$(run postgres "$IMG" --version)
echo "$out"
echo "$out" | grep -q "$POSTGRES_VERSION" || fail "postgres is not $POSTGRES_VERSION"
pass "postgres $POSTGRES_VERSION"

out=$(run patroni "$IMG" --version)
echo "$out"
echo "$out" | grep -q "$PATRONI_VERSION" || fail "patroni is not $PATRONI_VERSION"
pass "patroni $PATRONI_VERSION"

out=$(run pgbackrest "$IMG" version)
echo "$out"
echo "$out" | grep -q "${PGBACKREST_APT_VERSION%%-*}" || fail "pgbackrest is not ${PGBACKREST_APT_VERSION%%-*}"
pass "pgbackrest ${PGBACKREST_APT_VERSION%%-*}"

run pg_rewind "$IMG" --version >/dev/null || fail "pg_rewind missing"
pass "pg_rewind present"

run tini "$IMG" --version >/dev/null || fail "tini missing"
pass "tini present"

echo "ALL CHECKS PASSED"
EOF
chmod +x tests/image-build.sh
PATRONI_VERSION=4.1.5 is already in versions.env, so the test reads it from there.

5. Write the build script

tee scripts/image-build.sh > /dev/null <<'EOF'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Build the Postgres + Patroni + pgBackRest image from pinned versions.
set -euo pipefail
cd "$(dirname "$0")/.."
# shellcheck source=/dev/null
source versions.env

docker build -f image/Dockerfile \
  --build-arg BASE_IMAGE="$POSTGRES_BASE_IMAGE" \
  --build-arg PGBACKREST_APT_VERSION="$PGBACKREST_APT_VERSION" \
  --build-arg PATRONI_VERSION="$PATRONI_VERSION" \
  --build-arg POSTGRES_VERSION="$POSTGRES_VERSION" \
  -t "${IMAGE_NAME}:${IMAGE_TAG}" .
EOF
chmod +x scripts/image-build.sh
shellcheck scripts/image-build.sh tests/image-build.sh && echo "shellcheck OK"

7. Build the image
